# Progress log — in plain English

What has been built, what changed, and what is next. Newest first. Kept deliberately readable: if you only read one file to follow this project, read this one.

## 24 November 2026 (Phase 12 — code-review fix B2: the database store now locks its writes)

- The bug, in plain words: the database store held one connection that any thread could use at once, with nothing serialising the writes. The server answers requests from many threads at the same time, so two ingests could interleave — one halfway through rewriting the tables while another changed the memory those tables are written from. Reproduced before fixing: six threads sending sixty alerts each raised errors on most ingests (316 to 328 of 360 across four runs), the errors arriving after the change had already landed in memory, and the file came back missing records the memory held (360 evidence records in memory, 358 after a reload).
- The fix: the store now holds one lock across each change and the write that persists it — an ingestion, a save, an approval being recorded — so a change and its persistence are a single step no other thread can cut into. Nothing else about the store's behaviour changes.
- Two new tests pin it: the same six-thread load now raises nothing and a reload returns exactly what memory holds (this test fails on the old code), and a write that fails leaves the file holding the last whole state, with the next good write bringing file and memory back into agreement.
- Measured after the fix: 485 tests pass (483 before, plus the two new ones), style and type checks clean, and every scored gate unchanged — scenarios 18 of 18, regression PASS, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10, proposal selection 9 of 9, 0 unsafe actions.
- The code-review report now marks B2 fixed; 22 of its 25 findings remain, and which one is fixed next is Abraham's call. The Gemini real-model score is still the one unchecked Phase 12 item, still awaiting his decision.

## 17 November 2026 (Phase 12 — the carry is closed, and the relaunched run is alive with failing calls) — alive is not the same as healthy

- **The last batch landed.** Its push card was allowed through, its commit sits on top of the merge, and the public plan and progress log now match the local copies exactly. Nothing is being carried — the long run of stranded hourly entries is closed.
- **The repository is quiet, as it should be.** It was read back with no open issues and no open pull requests. The first contributor’s four pieces stay complete.
- **Tonight’s scoring relaunch started on time, and it is still running.** The start step fired at 00:50 Pacific and is now spent, as a used one-off step is; its record shows the runner started as its own process and was seen alive about a minute later. At this check, about nineteen minutes in, that same process is still alive with a call in flight, and its log — written as it happens this time — is growing: empty at the start, 2,928 bytes a quarter of an hour in, 5,056 bytes at the last look. Yesterday’s run left an empty log and no process; this one has not died that way.
- **Then the machine itself was replaced, and the run died with it.** Minutes after the checks above, the computer this work runs on was swapped for a fresh one (its clock shows a new start at 09:21:48 UTC, about a minute old when checked). The runner is gone — no such process exists on the new machine — and its log is final: 10,180 bytes, last written at 09:19 UTC, holding fourteen failed calls in all (six service-busy, seven usage-limit, one helper error) and no results file. So tonight’s run, like yesterday’s, produced no score — with the cause known this time: it lived and worked for about twenty-nine minutes and was killed by the machine replacement, while its calls were failing throughout. This pass did not restart it: starting again is Abraham’s decision. The watchdog look at 03:50 Pacific and the collect step at 08:50 Pacific both survived the replacement and are still set, and the collect step owns the formal report. One more plain fact follows: a separate process survives the session that started it, not the machine it runs on — a run that must last the night needs a home that is not replaced underneath it.
- **But its calls are failing, and that is stated plainly.** The log’s first seven finished calls all failed after being retried — five were turned away with a service-busy answer (HTTP 503), one hit the usage limit (HTTP 429), and one failed inside the calling helper. A failed call gives its incident no claims at all, by the agent’s own design, so those calls cannot earn their scenarios. There is no results file yet, which is expected partway through a run of about one and a half to two hours, and there is no score. This pass changed nothing the run uses, restarted nothing, and made no Gemini calls of its own. The watchdog look at 03:50 Pacific (report only) and the collect-and-publish step at 08:50 Pacific are both still set: the collect step publishes only from a complete results file, must state any failed-call count alongside the scores, and publishes nothing if the failures are heavy.
- **One plain lesson about watching a run.** Alive is not the same as healthy. A check after a start looks at three separate facts — is the process there, is its log growing, and how many of its calls have failed — because a run can fail with no process and an empty log, as yesterday’s did, or with a live process whose calls keep failing, as today’s is doing so far. Neither early state settles the finished run; the completed results file is what gets judged.

## 16 November 2026 (Phase 12 — the launch happened, and the runner is gone) — alive at one minute is not running at fifteen

- **The scheduled real-model run launched — and then died silently.** The launch job fired at 00:50 Pacific and started the Gemini scoring run detached (process 19271, model gemini-3.8-flash); about 70 seconds later it was verified alive, with its first call already in flight and its log file created. This pass, about a quarter of an hour after the launch, finds no runner process and no helper process, the log still empty (0 bytes) and no results file. A full run takes about an hour and a half to two hours, so the run did not finish — it stopped sometime after its first minute, before printing or writing anything, and it left no error behind. No real-model score exists, and nothing is published.
- **This pass did not restart it, on purpose.** The dead run may already have spent part of today's free quota, and a second launch from a different job could spend it twice or collide with the first. The collect step is still scheduled for 08:55 Pacific today; it is charged with reading the log and reporting the failure honestly. Whether to relaunch — tonight on a fresh quota, or another way — is Abraham's decision, and this finding is reported for it.
- **One plain lesson about silent runners.** A runner that prints only at the end leaves no evidence when it dies early. Alive at one minute is not the same as running at fifteen minutes: the first check after a launch has to look for the process itself, and an empty log with no process and no results after the launch time means the run died silently — never that it is still running.

## 14 November 2026 (Phase 12, item 3 complete) — the demo now diagnoses the fault you actually injected

- **Phase 12's third item is complete: in the Docker Compose demo, the agent's diagnosis reads the demo's own live Prometheus, not seeded demo data.** The Compose file now runs the agent through a new live factory (`sre_agent.demo_live:create_live_demo_app`) instead of the shipped app. Its metrics come from the stack's live Prometheus, its service status is worked out from that same Prometheus (failing when the live error count is above zero), and its log lines come from the sample service itself.
- **The sample service learned to keep a log.** It already counted every request; now it also records one log line per request — a failing request's line carries the same "downstream call timed out" detail its reply carries — and serves them at `GET /logs`, keeping only the newest 100. So when you run `./demo/inject_fault.sh`, the five failing requests are the five error lines the diagnosis reads, and the error count in its evidence is the live count Prometheus scraped — not the seeded picture's made-up 162.
- **The honest proof is the quiet case.** The new tests (`tests/test_demo_live.py`) run a fake Prometheus with test-chosen values next to the real sample service: with the failure on, the live diagnosis names that fault and its evidence carries the live count (3, in the test); with no failure recorded, the same live tools say "I don't know" — where the seeded tools would always diagnose their made-up picture. No seeded adapters and no seeded memory are wired into the live mode, for the same reason: extra evidence there would have to be live too, or not present.
- **One security rule needed a named exception, and it got one — narrowly.** Prometheus inside Compose is at `http://prometheus:9090`: plain http to a host that is not this machine, which the backend address rules (finding F-05) refused — rightly, by default. The rules now carry a separate list, `allow_http_hosts`, for exactly this case: a private container network on the operator's own machine, a service that takes no credential, and the operator naming that one host on purpose. It is a different list from the host allow-list on purpose — that list still cannot widen the http rule — the default is unchanged, and any other host is still refused. Tests pin all three facts.
- **What did not change, stated plainly:** the shipped app the README walkthrough uses is still the seeded demo server; the hosted demo is untouched (seeded, Tier-3-only, by design); and a fix approved in the Compose demo still runs against the seeded demo workloads, not the sample container — the sample service stops failing only when you switch its chaos switch off. That remaining caveat is still in the demo guide; the seeded-diagnosis caveat is out of it, because it is no longer true. And a full `docker compose up` run still needs Docker, which the build environment does not have — the wiring is pinned as text and the live path is run against the stack's own pieces over real HTTP, and the guide says so.
- Everything verified locally in full: 468 tests pass (up from 457), style and type checks clean (type checks now cover 37 source files), and every scored check is unchanged — scenarios 18 of 18, regression gate passed, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10, proposal selection 8 of 8, 0 unsafe actions. A package built from a clean copy carries the new live module and no leftover old files.
- **Bookkeeping note, in the open:** at this pass's start the public copy was still at the adapters-delete commit — the previous pass's security-review batch was verified locally but had not landed, the same stranding found twice before. This pass's batch therefore carries those files together with its own work, and is judged only by reading the public copy back; the old `security_review.py` is then removed from the public copy with one separate delete, package first.
- **What remains in Phase 12:** one item in Abraham's order — the starter issues for contributors (with the single dev-setup block in the contributing guide, and Discussions enabled). The first item (the bigger scenario suite) still waits only on its real-model score, which needs an operator-supplied key.

## 14 November 2026 (Phase 12, item 2 complete) — the security review is split: probes in one module, their findings in another

- **Phase 12's second item is complete: the code restructure is done.** Its last slice split the security review — the agent's self-attack probes and the findings they cannot prove away — which all lived in one file, `security_review.py`, at 826 lines. That file is now a package: `findings.py` holds the findings (the data: the eight written-down weaknesses, F-01 to F-08), and `probes.py` holds the ten probes and the code that runs them.
- **Nothing changed in how anything works.** The findings list and every probe were moved word for word — after the split, each was compared against the original file and is identical. The package hands out exactly the names the old file did, so every `from sre_agent.security_review import ...` in the project works exactly as before. Three new tests pin that: the package is a package, each half lives in its own module, and the names you get from the package are the very same objects as the ones in the two halves.
- **One change was necessary, and it is stated, not hidden.** One probe checks that no part of the agent can run a shell, by scanning the project's own code files. It found those files by looking in the folder its own file sat in — move the file, and it would have scanned its new small folder and checked almost nothing. It now scans the whole project package, folder by folder, and skips only its own file. That also fixes a quiet gap: the old scan looked only at the top level, so it had already stopped covering the read adapters after they were split into their own package.
- **Why bother, in plain words:** the findings are now one small file a reader can open on their own — the list of what is still wrong, with nothing else around it — and the probes are another. And because the move changed no behaviour, the bigger scenario suite from the first Phase 12 item acts as the safety net: if any score had moved, the move would have been wrong.
- No score moved. Everything verified locally in full: 457 tests pass (up from 454), style and type checks clean, and the scored checks are unchanged — scenarios 18 of 18, regression gate passed, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10, proposal selection 8 of 8, 0 unsafe actions everywhere. A package built from a clean copy was also checked: it carries the new package files, no leftover old file, and runs the probes 10 of 10 on its own.
- **What remains in Phase 12:** two items, in Abraham's order — wiring the demo to live Prometheus next, then the starter issues for contributors. The first item (the bigger scenario suite) still waits only on its real-model score, which needs an operator-supplied key.
- Upload: this pass pushes its changed files as one batch and checks each one by reading the public copy back afterwards. The old `security_review.py` cannot travel in that batch — the upload route cannot delete files — so it is removed from the public copy with one separate delete right after the batch lands, package first, so the public copy is never left without a working security review import.

## 13 November 2026 (Phase 12 begins) — the scenario suite grows from five faults to eighteen, and which fix a diagnosis points to is now scored

- **Phase 12's first item is under way, in Abraham's order: the bigger suite first, as the safety net for everything after it.** The chaos suite grew from 5 scenarios to 18. Every scenario now expects a *distinct* top cause, and the specific cause is scored in every scenario — before this pass, two pairs of scenarios shared one expected cause, and disk fill, config error and bad deploy were scored only on seeing a failure, not on naming its cause.
- **To name those causes honestly, the deterministic backend learned to read symptoms in the error logs.** It now checks a fixed list of symptom signals (`LOG_SIGNALS` in `model_backend.py`) — a full disk, a bad configuration value, running out of memory, an image that cannot be pulled, a crash loop, a database deadlock, an exhausted connection pool, failing name resolution, a TLS certificate check, a permission check, rate limiting, a refused connection, a recent deploy, a backed-up queue, an unavailable dependency — each with the backend's own fixed wording and the log evidence cited. A log line can raise one of those fixed wordings; it can never add wording, an action, or a target of its own. Where a cause is named from log wording alone, with no dedicated tool behind it (disk, config, deploy), the scenario now says exactly that, instead of claiming the cause cannot be named at all.
- **The new scenarios** are: out of memory, connection refused, DNS failure, TLS certificate error, database deadlock, connection pool exhausted, rate limited, permission error, image pull failure, crash loop, queue backlog, dependency unavailable, and a generic error-rate case (requests failing with no named symptom, scored on the generic cause). A test pins that all eighteen expected causes, and all eighteen measured top causes, are distinct.
- **Which fix a diagnosis points to is now measured too.** A new proposal-selection suite (`proposal_selection.py`) runs each case's real diagnosis first, then scores a small conservative selector: a recent deploy with a previous version selects a rollback, missing copies select a scale, a timeout, a crash loop or a generic failure selects a restart — and every other named cause selects *nothing*, because no runbook in the library fixes a full disk, a bad setting, or someone else's rate limit, so the honest answer is to escalate. Measured: 8 of 8 correct selections, 0 unsafe actions. The selector is advice only, like a memory suggestion: it runs nothing, proposes nothing through the policy engine, and is not wired into the server or the CLI. It is scored first, before any wiring is considered.
- **The per-backend scores were re-measured on the bigger suite, and one of them fell — published as it fell.** The deterministic backend scores 18 of 18 and passes all gates. The scripted test-double client behind the language-model backend scores 3 of 18 (17%), fails the regression gate, and does not pass all gates: its rules know only three causes, and a suite that scores the specific cause everywhere shows exactly that. Its injection score is unchanged (8 of 8, 0 unsafe). The evaluation page and the operator guide now carry these numbers; the double was never a real model and was never recommended, and this pass changes neither fact.
- **One honest widening, stated where it belongs:** the injection set's known limitation used to name one symptom word ("timeout"). It now names the whole signal list, in `injection.py`, the evaluation page, and the plan: a crafted log line carrying a symptom word can raise that fixed hypothesis — signal spoofing, not instruction following — and the set still passes 8 of 8.
- **What this pass did not do:** the real-model backend score. Publishing one needs an operator-supplied key and the exact client named; no key was supplied and no real model was called, so Phase 12's first item stays *unchecked* until that step is done. The baseline did not move either: the regression gate passes against the recorded v0.2.0 baseline, with the thirteen new scenarios noted as new; they join the record only at the next release, on purpose.
- Everything verified locally in full: 447 tests pass (up from 438: the new proposal suite and the expanded evaluation pins), style and type checks clean, and the scored checks are: scenarios 18 of 18, regression gate passed, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10, proposal selection 8 of 8, 0 unsafe actions everywhere.
- **One file is held, on purpose:** `quickstart.sh`'s wording was updated locally (it now says eighteen scenarios), but it is *not* in this pass's batch — the batch route cannot carry a file's executable bit, and that bit is what broke the owner's first run before. It waits for Abraham's next real git push, the same holding pattern the publishing workflow used.
- Upload: this pass pushes its changed files as one batch and checks each one by reading the public copy back afterwards.

## 13 November 2026 (Phase 11 continues) — the publishing package is ready: workflow prepared, steps written, artifacts proven

- **Phase 11's last item is now down to two maintainer steps.** Everything that does not need Abraham's own accounts is built and proven in this pass: a release workflow that publishes to PyPI and GHCR, a plain-English guide with the exact steps, and a built package that passes the publisher's own checks. Its PyPI job uses trusted publishing: PyPI is told in advance which repository, workflow file and environment may publish `sreagent`, and GitHub proves the run's identity with a short-lived token — no PyPI API token exists in this project, and none must be added. Its GHCR job uses that one run's own GitHub token to push the public-safe hosted demo image, `ghcr.io/kakoozajerry/sre-agent-demo`, built from `Dockerfile.hosted` — the build that refuses to start without a token. The plain local server image is deliberately not published: it may run token-less, which is right on one machine and wrong for a public image.
- **Every step the workflow will run was run here first.** The file parses as YAML with exactly the two jobs, triggers and permissions intended; `python -m build` produced `sreagent-0.2.0` as both a wheel and a source distribution, with the metadata read back from the wheel itself; and `twine check` — the check PyPI uploads are judged by — passed both files. Four new tests pin the package's facts (438 total, up from 434): trusted publishing with no stored token, the GHCR image name and file, and the guide's exact values.
- **The guide (`docs/publishing.md`) carries the steps word for word, because PyPI checks them word for word.** The pending-publisher values are: project `sreagent`, owner `KakoozaJerry`, repository `sre-agent`, workflow filename `publish.yml`, environment `pypi` — one different word and the first publish fails. The guide ends in read-backs, not a green tick: the PyPI record answering 200, `pipx install sreagent` working in a clean place, and the GHCR package pulled and set public.

A normal recording would be a GIF, but a GIF is a binary file and the upload route that publishes this repository cannot carry binary files — text can. Output lines are shortened for the screen, the README says so next to the recording, and the pauses between screens are reading pauses, not a claim about how fast the agent is.

No step is allowed to fail quietly.

## 13 November 2026 (Phase 11 continues) — a security policy, and why the demo token is public

- This pass builds Phase 11's security-policy item: **the repository now has a `SECURITY.md` at its root.** Until now the way to report a security problem lived in one paragraph of the contributing guide; a visitor looking for the standard place — the file GitHub itself points to — found nothing there.
- What the policy says, in plain words: report a problem privately first, to the maintainer through his GitHub profile, and do not open a public issue with the working details — the same path the contributing guide already gave, now in the standard place, and with no invented email address (the project has none for this). Only the latest release (0.2.0) is supported with security fixes; older releases are asked to upgrade. The scope is written both ways: in scope are ways past the guardrails the project claims — the tiers, the single-use approvals, the pinned target, the caps, a token bypass when one is set, an instruction planted in telemetry that changes a wording, a target or an action; out of scope are the things that are already public by design or already on record — the hosted demo's public token, a local server run with no token (documented as local-only), a restatement of a known finding from the security review with nothing new added, other people's services, and the pretend demo data itself.
- The second half of the item is in the hosted-demo guide: **a new section explains why the hosted demo's token is public.** The token is printed in the README on purpose, so the guide now gives the four reasons in one place — the demo is read-mostly, it holds no real data, the Tier 3 demo grant is the only action that can run, and the ingestion caps are on — and states the limit of that reasoning: a server holding real incidents needs a real, private token. `SECURITY.md` carries the short version and links to the guide's full one, the README's document list links `SECURITY.md`, and the contributing guide now points at the policy too.
- Two new tests pin the policy and the rationale — the reporting path, the supported version, the scope sections, and the guide's four reasons — so a later edit cannot quietly drop them. All 414 tests pass (up from 412), the style and type checks are clean, and every scored check is unchanged: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passed, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10 — 0 unsafe actions.
- The lesson from this pass, now written in the plan: a security policy must use the reporting path that actually exists — an invented address in the standard file would be worse than the honest one, because reports would go nowhere while looking sent.

- How it works now, in plain words: a small new part of the code (`evaluation_page.py`) reads the release record — the recorded score file the regression gate checks against, plus that release's own walkthrough — and writes the score block on the evaluation page from those files, between markers that say the block is generated. Running `python -m sre_agent.evaluation_page` regenerates it. If a number it needs is missing from the record, it fails with an error instead of guessing. The block now reads, correctly: release v0.2.0, recorded 12 November 2026, 358 tests on release day, scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passed, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10, 0 unsafe actions. One honest point is stated in the block itself: those are release-day numbers, frozen at the release — the test suite has grown since (412 tests now), and the count on the page moves only when a new release is recorded and the block is regenerated.
- A test now checks the page against the record: if a future release changes the record and nobody regenerates the page, the tests fail. Six more tests check the generation itself — that it reads the record, says exactly what the record says, fails loudly when a number is missing, and changes nothing when run twice. All 412 tests pass (up from 405), the style and type checks are clean, and every scored check is unchanged: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passed, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10 — 0 unsafe actions.
- The lesson from this pass, now written in the plan: a number that must stay true should be produced from its record and pinned by a test, not re-typed.

Render built the repo's own Blueprint and the first deploy went live from commit 07a9540; `/health` was checked from outside and answers {"status":"ok"}.

## 13 November 2026 (Phase 10 continues) — the deployment package is ready; deploying is Abraham's sign-in step

- This pass takes Phase 10's deployment item as far as it can honestly go without Abraham's own hosting account: **the host is chosen and its cost is named here, before anything is paid for, and everything that does not need his sign-in is built, verified, and in the repo.** The item itself stays unchecked — there is still no public URL — because claiming a deployment from a package would be claiming a result nobody measured.
- **Host and cost, named first: Render, a free web service, $0.** Nothing is paid and no card is needed. The free plan's limits are stated in the guide before anyone deploys: a free service sleeps after 15 minutes with no traffic (the next visitor waits about a minute for it to wake), and a workspace gets 750 free instance hours a month. Fine for a demo; not production hosting.
- What is in the repo now: `render.yaml`, a Render Blueprint naming one service (`sre-agent-demo`, Oregon, free plan, Docker), with Render's own health check set to watch `/health` once the service is live; and `Dockerfile.hosted`, the hosted image — it installs from the hash-checked lock file, runs as a user who is not root, and starts the hosted demo factory (the build that refuses to start without a token) on the port Render assigns. The demo token is never in the repo: the Blueprint marks it `sync: false`, so Render asks Abraham to type its value when he applies it.
- The package was verified the one way this environment allows. There is no Docker here, so the image itself was not built; instead its exact steps were run by hand — lock install with hashes checked, package install, hosted factory started with a token on a chosen port — and a visitor's whole path was run over real HTTP against that server: `/health` answered, a request with no token was refused, the demo alert was diagnosed, the pre-approved restart ran to `mitigating`, and the resolved alert closed the incident.
- The lesson from this pass, now written in the plan: a deployment that needs the owner's own account splits cleanly at the sign-in. Everything before it is buildable and testable in the repo; everything after it is the owner's short manual step — the same shape as the GitHub Releases, which were also a human step on the website, and were not claimed until they existed.
- **The one step left is Abraham's:** sign in to Render, choose New → Blueprint, select this repository, type a token value when asked, and apply. The guide ([hosted-demo.md](hosted-demo.md)) writes those steps down in order. When the service is live, its public HTTPS address goes in this log, and the README gains its "Try the hosted demo" link — the last Phase 10 item, which can only point somewhere real once the address exists.
- Four new tests pin the package (402 total, up from 398): the Blueprint's service, plan and health check, the token never being written in it, the image running the hosted factory on the host's port as a non-root user, and the guide naming the host, the cost, the limits and the remaining step. The style and type checks are clean, and every scored check is unchanged: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passed, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10 — 0 unsafe actions.

## 12 November 2026 (Phase 10 starts) — a public-safe demo mode

- Phase 10 — the hosted demo — has started, and this pass builds the piece that has to come before any public address: **a public-safe demo mode**. It is one server build (`create_hosted_demo_app` in `hosted_demo.py`) with every safety rule switched on at once, in one place, so a later deployment cannot quietly forget one of them. The guide is [hosted-demo.md](hosted-demo.md).
- A token is required, with no fallback: the server is not built at all without one. Building it with no token fails with an error, instead of using the unauthenticated mode the local server allows. With a token set, every page except `/health` needs it.
- It only ever sees seeded demo data: the demo diagnosis data, the demo past incidents, and the demo workloads. Nothing real is read or touched, and there are no real credentials anywhere in it — the token is the only secret.
- The Tier 3 demo grant is the only action that can run. Restart and scale in the demo's shop area, touching at most three copies at once, are pre-approved and run at once. Anything that would need a person's approval cannot run here at all: the approval path is switched off in the server for this mode, so a rollback is refused, leaves nothing waiting, and there is no approval page for anyone to use. That is deliberate — a public demo should not hand out approvals.
- The size limits stay on: at most 100 alerts in one payload, capped labels and summaries, at most 200 evidence records per incident and 1,000 incidents held.
- A visitor's whole path was checked in tests: send the demo alert, read the diagnosis, run the pre-approved restart, and close the incident with a resolved alert. Seven new tests cover that path and the refusals (no token, wrong token, rollback, oversize payload). All 396 tests pass, the style and type checks are clean, and every scored check is unchanged: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passed, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10 — 0 unsafe actions.
- Honest limit, stated plainly: this is a mode, not a deployment. There is no public URL yet, no host has been chosen, and nothing has been paid for. Those are the next Phase 10 steps, and the host and any cost will be named here before anything is paid for.
- Upload: this pass pushes its changed files as one batch and checks each one by reading the public copy back afterwards.

## 12 November 2026 (Phase 9 is complete) — each backend now has its own scores

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: a check at the start of this pass found the public copy at the single commit that carried the whole Phase 7, Phase 8 and Phase 9 batch and the v0.2.0 release. Nothing is waiting any more, so this pass's upload is only its own work, checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 9's third and last item: **the chaos, regression and prompt-injection suites must pass with the model backend enabled before it is recommended, and scores are published separately for each backend.** Before, every suite ran only with the plain-rules backend, so every published score was that backend's and a model backend simply had none. Now each suite can be run with any diagnosis backend switched on, every report names the backend that earned its scores, and a new scoring step (`score_backend`, in `backend_scores.py`) runs all three suites for one backend and says, in plain words, whether that backend passes all the gates: every chaos scenario right, the regression gate passed against the same recorded scores every backend is compared with, every hostile-text case passed, and no unsafe actions anywhere. Scoring never moves the recorded scores — a backend cannot get an easier gate by being scored.
- The scores, measured in this pass and published in the evaluation guide as a table with one row per backend, never mixed together: the **deterministic** (plain-rules) backend scores 5 of 5 on the chaos scenarios, passes the regression gate, and passes 8 of 8 hostile-text cases, with 0 unsafe actions — it passes all the gates, and it stays the recommended backend and the default. The **language-model** backend, switched on with a scripted test-double client, scores the same: 5 of 5, regression gate passed, 8 of 8, 0 unsafe actions — it passes all the gates *for that test double*.
- The test double needs explaining, because it decides what those second scores mean. It is a small scripted client that lives in the tests, not in the program: it reads only the prompt it is given — the evidence list, exactly what a real model's client would see — and answers with fixed wordings chosen by simple rules over the evidence summaries (how many copies are ready, whether the logs mention a timeout, how many error lines matched). It is not a real model and it calls nothing. Its passing scores prove two real things: the scoring run works end to end with a model backend switched on, and the checks on a model's replies hold under all three suites. They do not prove that any real model diagnoses well. The same tests prove the scoring can fail: a client that always errors scores 0 of 5 and fails the gates with the drop named, and a client that repeats a hostile order as its answer fails the hostile-text set. The lesson, now written in the plan: a score belongs to the exact client that was scored, never to a backend in the abstract.
- Honest limits, stated wherever the scores are published: no real model was called anywhere in this pass, and **no real model is scored or recommended** — this project ships no real model client, and a person who wires their own must score that exact client this way and publish its scores separately before recommending it. Six of the eight hostile-text cases run the diagnosis step with the named backend; the other two go through alert intake only and involve no diagnosis backend at all. And no score for model-proposed fixes is published: which fix a proposer would choose is still not scored.
- Seven new tests pin all of this — the reports naming their backend, both backends' separate scores, the broken client failing, the scores belonging to the exact client, the order-repeating client failing, and the documents. All 389 tests pass, and the other measured gates did not move: Tier 3 drills 7 of 7 and security probes 10 of 10, with 0 unsafe actions. With this entry, Phase 9 is complete — and the roadmap has no unchecked items left.
- What comes next is a direction call for Abraham, like the Phase 7–9 direction was: the roadmap is finished, and an hourly pass will not invent a new phase to fill the hour.

## 12 November 2026 (Phase 9 continues) — a model may propose a fix, and it can never approve one

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: the waiting is over. A check at the start of this pass found the public copy at the last commit of the previous pass's upload, so everything built in Phase 7, Phase 8 and the start of Phase 9 is now public. This pass's upload is only its own work, and every file is checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 9's second item: **the policy engine, approval tiers and audit log apply identically to model-proposed actions — the model can propose, never approve.** A language model may now propose a fix, through a new, separate channel (`model_proposals.py`). It is off by default, like the model diagnosis backend: it exists only where a person builds a proposer around their own model client and calls it in their own code. No setting, flag or shipped wiring calls it, and a diagnosis reply still may not carry an action — proposals are asked for separately, on purpose.
- A proposal reply is checked as untrusted input, like everything else a model says. It must be one JSON object with only three things: a runbook name, its parameters, and a short reason. A reply carrying anything else — a target, an approver's name, a decision — is refused whole, so a model proposal cannot be aimed at a different service and cannot approve itself. A model that fails, answers badly, names a fix that does not exist, or gives parameters the fix does not take simply gets no proposal: nothing is written down and nothing runs, exactly as a proposal that cannot be built is refused on the server before the policy engine is asked.
- A proposal that passes those checks is then treated *exactly* like any other proposal — and that word is meant literally, because the same code does it. The proposal is built by the same `build_action` everyone uses (parameters checked, how many copies it touches counted from the workload's real state, target always the one pinned on the incident, and the reason marked so a reader can see a model proposed it). It is decided by the same policy engine, and that decision is written to the same audit log before anything can run. If it needs a human, it waits as the same pending action, with the same 15 minutes to decide; a named human approving it gets the same single-use yes, with the policy checked again at that moment. If the policy allows it outright — at Tier 3, a grant a human wrote into the policy in advance — it runs through the same executor. The lesson, now written in the plan: sameness must be literal, not a second copy of the rules that looks the same — a test compares a model proposal and a hand-built identical proposal side by side, and their decisions, reasons and audit records match field by field.
- The boundary in the item's title is built into the shape: the proposer has no approve step at all, and submitting a proposal can produce a *pending* action, never an approval. Approving is still only a named human, through the approval flow that already existed.
- Twelve new tests pin all of this — the Tier 2 wait with nothing run, the identical decision and audit record, a named human approving a model proposal exactly once, a Tier 3 run on the policy's own allow, too-big and halted proposals refused and written down, replies carrying a target or an approver refused whole, and the documents. All 382 tests pass, and the measured gates did not move: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes against the v0.2.0 record, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10.
- Honest limits: no real model was called anywhere in this pass (the tests use fake clients returning fixed replies, as before), model proposals are not offered on the server, and no score for model proposals is published yet. That scoring — the same suites run with the model backend enabled, with scores published separately for each backend — is Phase 9's last item, and the last item on the roadmap.
- Next, and last in Phase 9: run the chaos, regression and prompt-injection suites with the model backend enabled, and publish each backend's scores separately, before the model backend is ever recommended.

## 12 November 2026 (Phase 9 starts) — a language model may read the evidence, and it is off by default

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: the earlier passes' files (all of Phase 7's wiring work, the quickstart, the walkthrough, the v0.2.0 release and the community basics, thirty-four files in all) still have not reached GitHub — a check at the start of this pass found the public copy still at the commit before them. So this pass's upload carries those thirty-four files together with its own, and every file is checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 9's first item: **a language-model backend behind the same diagnosis report shape, off by default.** Before, the part that reads the gathered evidence and writes the claims and possible causes was fixed inside the diagnosis step — plain rules, written in code. Now that reading is a *backend* the diagnosis step is handed (`model_backend.py`): the step gathers evidence exactly as before, the backend reads it, and the report that comes back has the same shape either way — and now names the backend that produced it.
- The plain-rules backend is now a named thing, the deterministic backend, and it stays the default everywhere: the demo, the command-line tool, the scored suites and the server program this project ships all use it unless someone chooses otherwise. Its rules did not change — they moved, word for word, and every score measured in this pass is the score it measured before.
- The new backend asks a language model instead. It exists only where a person wires it in their own code, around their own model client — any small object with one method that takes a prompt and returns text — and hands it to the diagnosis step or to the server. There is no setting, no environment variable and no command-line flag for it, and this project ships no real model client: which provider, model and key to use is the operator's own decision. The prompt it sends is built only from the evidence records gathered for the incident (their ids, kinds, exact queries and summaries — already cleaned of secrets and kept to a fixed size), under instruction text fixed in the code.
- A model's answer is treated as untrusted input, like the log lines it reads, and checked before any of it can become a claim: the answer must be one JSON object with only two keys (claims and possible causes); each entry may carry only a statement, the evidence ids behind it and a confidence between 0 and 1; every id it cites must be a real evidence record for that incident. An entry that carries anything else — a runbook name smuggled in beside a claim is the test case — is dropped. A reply with an extra top-level field (an `actions` key is the test case) is refused whole. A client that fails, or an answer that is not JSON, simply leaves the backend with nothing to say, and the incident is handed to a human, exactly as it is when the evidence is thin. The lesson, now written in the plan: a backend's output needs the same evidence checks as any other input — the loop's own rule that nothing may cite evidence that is not on the incident is the backstop, and the model backend's stricter checks sit in front of it so a bad answer degrades to "I don't know" instead of failing the diagnosis.
- Twelve new tests pin all of this — the default being deterministic and unchanged, a fake model client (a test double returning fixed replies; no real model was called anywhere in this pass) diagnosing behind the same report shape, unknown evidence dropped, smuggled fields dropped or refused, a broken model escalating cleanly, and a server using the model backend only when one is handed to it while the shipped server stays deterministic. All 370 tests pass, and the measured gates did not move: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes against the v0.2.0 record, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10.
- Honest limits, stated wherever this is described: the model backend proposes wording inside a diagnosis only — it proposes no action, and the next Phase 9 item (the policy engine treating model-proposed actions exactly like any other, with the model never able to approve) is not built or claimed here; and no score for the model backend is published yet — every published score is the deterministic backend's, and the model backend must pass the same suites with its scores published separately before it is ever recommended, which is Phase 9's last item.
- Next in Phase 9: the policy engine, approval tiers and audit log applying identically to model-proposed actions — the model can propose, never approve.


## 12 November 2026 (Phase 8 is complete) — v0.2.0 is released, and the door is open

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: the earlier passes' files (all of Phase 7's wiring work, the quickstart and the walkthrough, twenty-one files in all) still have not reached GitHub — a check at the start of this pass found the public copy still at the commit before them. So this pass's upload carries those twenty-one files together with its own, and every file is checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 8's third and last item: **the v0.2.0 release, with its changelog and the community basics.** The version is now 0.2.0 in the program and in the project file, and the recorded scores the regression gate checks against were re-recorded on purpose at the new version — but only after the fresh run had first passed against the old v0.1.0 record unchanged, so the new record (which now keeps three releases' scores) hides no drop.
- The changelog (`CHANGELOG.md`, new at the top of the project) tells the whole story since v0.1.0 in plain words: the security hardening (four findings fixed; two — the secret cleaning and the demo images — improved, not closed, and written down as exactly that), the live path being wired (the server diagnosing its own incidents, approvals served over HTTP, the read adapters and the memory feeding a diagnosis, a database file that survives a restart), and this polish phase. The release notes (`docs/v0.2.0-walkthrough.md`) print the scores measured today and the limits that ship with the release — among them that a server with no token set still has no authentication and stays a local demo, that the live adapters are still untested against real systems, and that creating the release and its tag on the GitHub website is a short step a person does by hand, because the connection used to build this project has no tool for it — the same honest note v0.1.0 carried.
- The community basics are the other half of the item: a contributing guide (`CONTRIBUTING.md`) that says how to set up, lists the real checks every change must pass, and states the rules that do not bend — no free-form commands, telemetry is untrusted input, evidence or it did not happen, a human approves, nothing claimed that was not measured, plain documentation — plus a private way to report a security problem instead of a public issue. A short code of conduct (`CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md`), two issue templates (the feature one asks how an idea stays safe and how it would be checked), and a pull request template that asks for what each check actually printed. The README links them all, in a new Contributing section and in its document list.
- The lesson from this pass, now written in the plan: a changelog or a guide written from intention drifts, the same way a walkthrough written before its run would. So the templates and the guide were checked against what the program's help and checks really print, and the changelog only repeats scores that were measured in this pass.
- The one-command install was proved again for the new version: a fresh copy with no private Python folder ran `./quickstart.sh` to the end, printing version 0.2.0 and the five scenarios at 5 of 5. Five new tests pin the changelog, the release notes, the contributing guide, the templates and the README's links to them. All 358 tests pass, and the measured gates on release day: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes against the new v0.2.0 record, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10 — 0 unsafe actions. With this entry, Phase 8 is complete.
- Next, and last on the roadmap: Phase 9 — a language-model backend behind the same diagnosis report shape, off by default, with its own published scores before it is ever recommended.


## 12 November 2026 (Phase 8 continues) — the walkthrough, run before it was written

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: the previous passes' files (all of Phase 7's wiring work and the quickstart, twenty-one files in all) still have not reached GitHub — a check at the start of this pass found the public copy still at the commit before them. This pass's work folds into files already in that set, so this pass's upload is the same twenty-one files, and every file is checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 8's second item: **a walkthrough in the README's Quick start — one alert, end to end.** Before, the README listed the commands but never took a new reader through the live path in order, even though the quickstart script already pointed readers at a walkthrough that was not there. Now the Quick start carries five plain steps: start the server this project ships, send it the demo checkout alert, read the diagnosis in the terminal, propose the restart fix and approve it under your own name, then close the incident with a "resolved" alert and stop the server. The operator guide points new readers at it too.
- Nothing in the walkthrough was written from intention. In this pass a fresh copy of the project was installed by the quickstart script itself, and the five steps were then run against that copy's real server, one by one. What actually happened is what the walkthrough prints: the alert was created and diagnosed at once ("Most likely: A call that checkout depends on is timing out"), with eight evidence records behind it — the alert, the service status, a log search, two metric queries, and the Kubernetes, Datadog and Azure answers — and the past-incident advice printed under its "advice only" label; the proposed restart waited for approval, touching 2 replicas, with 15 minutes to decide; approving it by name ran it ("Restarted checkout: restarts now 1, failing state cleared.") and left the incident at "mitigating", with the run itself as a ninth evidence record; and the resolved alert then closed it. The lesson, now written in the plan: a pointer to documentation that does not exist is a broken step, just like a broken command — so a walkthrough is written only from a run that really happened, with its real answers.
- Honest limits, stated in the walkthrough itself: it needs no Docker and touches no real system — the shipped server works from the seeded demo data; a run lands on "mitigating", not "resolved", because running a fix is not proof it worked; the shipped server keeps its incidents in memory, so they are gone when it stops; and the Docker Compose demo is a separate path, whose diagnosis still comes from the seeded data, not from its live Prometheus.
- Two new tests pin the walkthrough's steps and its honest-limit sentences. All 353 tests pass, and the measured gates did not move: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10.

## 12 November 2026 (Phase 7 continues) — incidents, diagnoses and approvals now survive a restart

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: the previous four passes' files (the server diagnosis, live approvals, read-adapters and memory-advice work, sixteen files in all) still have not reached GitHub — a check at the start of this pass found the public copy still at the commit before them, their uploads having timed out on the approval card. So this pass's upload carries those sixteen files together with its own, and every file is checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 7's fifth item: **a database store.** Before, everything a server knew lived in its own memory, so stopping it lost every incident — and any fix still waiting for a human's answer. Now a server built in code can use a database store instead (`SQLiteIncidentStore`, in the new `storage.py`): one SQLite file, written with only Python's built-in tools, so no new package was added.
- What is kept, in plain words: each incident with everything on it — its state, its evidence, its possible causes, its diagnosis, and any memory advice — plus how many repeat alerts it has counted and the rules for recognising them, so after a restart a repeated alert is still counted as a repeat (not a new incident), a related alert still joins its incident, and a "this is over" alert still closes it. Waiting approvals are kept in the same file: one that was waiting is still waiting after a restart, with the same deadline it was given — a restart does not give an old proposal extra time — and one that was already approved or turned down stays that way, so no fix can be run twice by restarting the server in between.
- The safety point is in what did not change. The database store does not re-write how alerts are handled: it inherits the in-memory store's code for all of that — repeats, grouping, closing, and the size limits — and only adds saving and loading underneath. The one new rule, now written in the plan: a change made to an incident outside alert handling (a diagnosis written on it, a fix run on it) is kept only when the caller hands it back to the store through its `save` step, and the server now does that after every diagnosis and every run, instead of assuming its changes were saved.
- Honest limits, stated wherever this is described: the file is for one operator and one server — it is not a shared database several servers write to at once — and the server program this project ships still uses the in-memory store, so the demo behaves exactly as before. A server's own stored, finished incidents are not yet fed back to it as memory; that was named as future work when memory was wired in, and it still is.
- Eight new tests pin all of this — seven for the store itself and the server round trip (a fresh store on the same file seeing the same incidents, repeats and grouping working after a restart, a diagnosis and its advice surviving, the size limits still applying, diagnose, propose, restart, approve once, restart again and find the decision and the "mitigating" state still there, with a second approval refused), and one pinning the operator guide's new database section. All 341 tests pass, and the measured gates did not move: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10.
- Next in Phase 7, and its last item: the evaluation gate, checked unchanged with the live path wired.


## 12 November 2026 (Phase 7 continues) — incident memory now advises live diagnoses

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: the previous three passes' files (the server diagnosis work, the live approvals work, and the read-adapters work, thirteen files in all) still have not reached GitHub — a check at the start of this pass found the public copy still at the commit before them, their uploads having timed out on the approval card. So this pass's upload carries those thirteen files together with its own, and every file is checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 7's fourth item: **past-incident memory now advises a live diagnosis.** Before, memory could only be looked at in its own demo (`sre-agent memory-demo`). Now an operator can hand a memory to the diagnosis step, or to the server, which passes it on. When a diagnosis finishes, the memory's report — similar past incidents, each runbook's record, and the runbooks it suggests — is attached to the incident itself, so the API shows it and `sre-agent show` prints it under a plain "advice only" label.
- The safety point is in the order things happen, and a test pins it: the advice is worked out **after** the diagnosis is decided, never before and never as an input to it. A diagnosis made with a memory attached is word for word the diagnosis made without one — same answer, same claims, same causes, same evidence, and no action record added. A suggestion is not a proposal: nothing waits for approval because memory spoke, and a suggested fix still has to be proposed and approved the normal way. And if the memory itself fails, its advice is simply skipped — a broken memory can never throw away a diagnosis.
- The server program this project ships now carries the seeded demo memory (the same four past incidents as `memory-demo`), so its demo diagnoses arrive with advice from that seeded past. No real system is touched, and a server built with a memory but no diagnosis step still only records incidents: there is no diagnosis to attach advice to. The memory report shapes now live with the other core records in the code, because an incident record can carry one — that is the only reason they moved.
- Nine new tests pin all of this — the advice arriving on a server diagnosis and in the `show` text, the diagnosis being unchanged by it, advice arriving even when an incident is handed to a human, a failing memory being skipped, no approval being created by a suggestion, and the shipped program carrying the demo advice. All 333 tests pass, and the measured gates did not move: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10.
- Next in Phase 7: a database store, so incidents, diagnoses and approvals survive a restart.


## 11 November 2026 (Phase 7 continues, later) — the read adapters now feed live diagnosis

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: the previous two passes' files (the server diagnosis work and the live approvals work, ten files in all) still have not reached GitHub — a check at the start of this pass found the public copy still at the commit before them, their uploads having timed out on the approval card. So this pass's upload carries those ten files together with its own, and every file is checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 7's third item: **the read adapters can now feed a live diagnosis.** Before, the Kubernetes, Datadog and Azure Monitor adapters could be looked at in their own demo, and their answers could become evidence records, but the diagnosis step never asked them anything. Now an operator can select any of them as extra evidence sources for the diagnosis step — one bundle (`AdapterSources`) handed to the diagnosis step itself, or to the server, which passes it on.
- How it works, in plain words. After its four usual questions, the diagnosis step asks each selected adapter exactly one fixed question: Kubernetes for the pods of the incident's service, Datadog for that service's error metric, and Azure Monitor for that service's error log rows. Each question counts as one step, the answers are stored on the incident as evidence like any other, and they can back a claim or a possible cause — but only as counts and measured values (how many pod containers are ready, how many restarts, the latest error value, how many error rows). Words from an adapter's answer — a pod's name, a log row's text — are never copied into a claim.
- The safety points are built into the shape. Which adapters are used is chosen by the operator when the server is built, never by anything in an alert or a log line. The Datadog and Azure questions are built only from a service name that is a plain name; a name carrying query syntax means that source is skipped for that incident, not built into a question. And because these are extra sources, their failures are gentler than the core plan's on purpose: an adapter that fails, or a step budget that runs out before an adapter's turn, skips that one source and keeps the diagnosis the main evidence already earned. An unfinished set of the four main questions still hands the incident to a human, exactly as before.
- The server program this project ships now selects the seeded demo adapters, matching its demo tool data, so a diagnosis from it carries Kubernetes, Datadog and Azure evidence from the same demo picture. No real system is touched, and no live adapter call is claimed as tested — that limit from Phase 4 has not moved. A server built with adapters but no tool layer still only records incidents: the adapters add to the diagnosis step, they do not replace it.
- Eight new tests pin all of this — adapters diagnosing a service the main tools see as quiet, healthy adapters adding evidence but no diagnosis, a broken adapter being skipped, the budget rule, a hostile service name never reaching a question, and the server paths including the shipped program. All 323 tests pass, and the measured gates did not move: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10.
- Next in Phase 7: incident memory in the live path.


## 11 November 2026 (Phase 7 continues) — approvals now live on the server too

- One piece of bookkeeping first, stated plainly: the previous pass's seven files (the server diagnosis work) did not reach GitHub — their first upload timed out and a check at the start of this pass confirmed the public copy is still at the commit before them. So this pass's upload carries those seven files together with its own, and every file is checked by reading the public copy back.
- This pass builds Phase 7's second item: **live approvals on the server.** Before, the only way to approve a fix was a demo command that did everything inside its own process. Now a server built with a policy and a runbook backend serves the same approval flow for its own incidents, over HTTP.
- How it works, in plain words. A fix is proposed for one diagnosed incident (`POST /incidents/<id>/actions`, naming a runbook and its parameters). The policy engine decides, and that decision is written in the server's audit log first. If the policy says a human must approve, the proposal waits as a pending approval, for at most 15 minutes. A named person then approves it or rejects it (`POST /incidents/<id>/approvals/<pending-id>/approve` or `.../reject`). Approving checks the policy again at that moment — if the environment was halted after the proposal, the approval fails and the refusal is written down. An approved fix runs exactly once; rejecting one runs nothing.
- Two safety points are built into the shape, not added on top. The proposal cannot name a target: it always acts on the target pinned on its incident, so there is no way to aim a fix at a different service through this route. And how many copies a fix touches is counted from the workload's real state, never taken from whoever proposes it.
- One honest limit, stated wherever this is described: a run that succeeds moves the incident to "mitigating", not "resolved". Running a fix is not the same as checking it worked, and the server does not check — the incident is resolved when a resolved alert arrives, as it always has been.
- The server program this project ships serves approvals against the seeded demo workloads, under a Tier 2 policy for the demo environment — no real system is touched. A server built without a policy or a backend holds no approvals, and its approval routes answer with a plain "not configured" message. There is no CLI command for the server's approvals yet; the CLI's `approve-demo` is still the in-process demo.
- Eleven new tests pin all of this — the pending approval's shape, the run happening once, a rejection running nothing, a proposal body that tries to name another target being ignored, the halt-at-approval-time refusal, an expired approval, bad proposals refused before any approval exists, the token lock covering these routes, and a server with no approval wiring holding none. All 315 tests pass, and the measured gates did not move: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10.
- Next in Phase 7: the read adapters feeding live diagnosis.


## 11 November 2026 (Phase 7 starts) — the server now diagnoses new incidents itself

- First, the direction is set. Abraham chose what comes after Phase 6: Phase 7 wires the live path (the diagnosis step inside the server, approvals on the server, the read adapters and memory feeding live diagnosis, and a database store), Phase 8 polishes the project for other users, and Phase 9 adds a language-model backend behind the same rules. The full list is in the plan's roadmap.
- Also good news on bookkeeping: the two log files that waited on an approval card through four checks have now landed in public, checked by reading them back. The public copy and the local copy agree in full again.
- This pass builds Phase 7's first item: **the diagnosis step now runs inside the server.** Before, the server only received alerts and filed them; the diagnosis step ran only in a separate demo command. Now, when a server is built with a tool layer, a firing alert that opens a new incident is diagnosed at once, as it arrives. The evidence the loop gathers, the possible causes it names, and its diagnosis are stored on the incident itself, so the API shows them and `sre-agent show` prints them.
- The rules around it, in plain words: an update grouped onto an incident that had been handed to a human is diagnosed again, fresh. A repeat alert is never diagnosed twice, and an incident that already has a diagnosis keeps it when another alert joins it. An alert saying the problem is over is never diagnosed. And if the diagnosis step itself breaks, the alert is still filed and the failure is written in the server's log — a broken diagnosis must never lose an alert.
- One honest limit, stated wherever this is described: the server program this project ships is built with the *demo* tool layer, so its diagnoses come from the seeded demo data and claim nothing about any real system. A real deployment hands the server its own tool layer when it builds it. A server built with no tool layer at all files incidents only, exactly as before — that stays the default, so no server starts producing demo diagnoses by accident.
- Nine new tests pin all of this, including that every cause the server stores cites evidence that is really on the incident, and that an unknown service is handed to a human instead of guessed at. All 304 tests pass, and the measured gates did not move: scenarios 5 of 5, regression gate passes, hostile-text set 8 of 8, Tier 3 drills 7 of 7, security probes 10 of 10.
- Next in Phase 7: live approvals on the server.

## 10 November 2026 (later) — the one file the secret scanner refused is fixed

The file as text no longer contains any token-shaped string — a scan for those shapes finds none — while the values the tests actually use are exactly the same as before, so the cleaning is tested against exactly the same shapes.
